========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, uh… I’m here because work’s been getting to me in a way it didn’t before. They made me the acting workflow lead in the warehouse, and ever since then I walk in already tense, like I’m bracing for something.

And it’s stupid little things sometimes—like SAP errors, stuff I used to just fix and move on from—I’ll snap about them now. I’m missing cycle-count deadlines too, which… I didn’t used to do. I’ve always been pretty solid with that kind of thing.

Then I get home and I’m just wiped out. I used to play basketball after work or mess around with my sketches, but lately I don’t even have it in me. It’s like I’m carrying the warehouse home with me.

Yeah… I mean, it’s mostly tied to work, I think. Like, I’ll be at home and still kind of carrying it with me, but the real “on edge” feeling starts before I even get into the warehouse.

At home it’s less like panic or something and more like I’m just drained. I used to go play basketball after work, or sit down and sketch for a bit—my brother used to joke my sketches all looked like warehouse layouts, actually—but lately I just don’t have the energy. So… yeah, not exactly all the time outside work, but it follows me home.

Detached… like, watching myself from outside or something? I don’t think so. Not really.

Sometimes at work I’ll kind of zone out for a second when there’s too much going on, like the scanner’s beeping and someone’s asking me about a pallet location, and I’m thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. That might just be me being distracted. My brother does this thing where he walks into a room and forgets why he’s there—he says it’s age, but he’s only two years older than me, so…

But no, I haven’t noticed feeling unreal or like I’m not in my body or anything like that.

No, I don’t think so. I’ve never had that feeling like I’m floating above myself or watching myself like a movie or anything.

I don’t know if I’d say worthless. Guilty… maybe sometimes? Like if something gets messed up at work, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder if I should’ve caught it sooner.

But it’s kind of hard to separate from just being worn out, you know?\n\nLike, my buddy asked me the other day if I wanted to catch a game, and I started thinking about parking and traffic and how late it would be, and then somehow I’m thinking about the receiving dock at work again. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around crying or anything. It’s more like everything feels heavier since that role got put on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m avoiding them exactly… I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do the job. But some stuff is definitely slipping.\n\nLike the cycle-count deadlines—my supervisor actually brought that up in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I’ve always been the guy who stays on top of that, so having it pointed out felt… bad.\n\nAnd then after work, it’s like I don’t switch back into being myself. I’ve stopped showing up for basketball, and I don’t really sit down to sketch like I used to. It’s not work tasks, I guess, but work is kind of taking over everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Detached… like, watching myself from outside or something? I don’t think so. Not really.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll kind of zone out for a second when there’s too much going on, like the scanner’s beeping and someone’s asking me about a pallet location, and I’m thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. That might just be me being distracted. My brother does this thing where he walks into a room and forgets why he’s there—he says it’s age, but he’s only two years older than me, so…\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed feeling unreal or like I’m not in my body or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Significant weight? I don’t think so. I mean, my pants still fit the same, as far as I can tell.\n\nI haven’t really been weighing myself, though. We used to have this scale in the bathroom, but the battery died and I just never replaced it—one of those little things you keep meaning to do and then forget. But no, I haven’t had anyone say I look like I’ve lost weight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never had that feeling like I’m floating above myself or watching myself like a movie or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll get caught up in my head sometimes, but that’s different, right? Like when you’re driving and suddenly you’re at the exit and you’re like, “Oh, I guess I drove here.” My dad used to call that highway hypnosis—he’d say it every time we went on long trips, which got old fast. Yeah… mostly with supervisors, I think. Coworkers too sometimes, but it’s more like when I walk into the warehouse now, I’m already tense before anything even happens.

Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m on edge all the time there. Like if someone puts something in wrong in SAP, even a little error, I’ll snap more than I mean to. And then I’m thinking about cycle counts I missed—stuff I didn’t used to miss before—and it just makes talking to people feel loaded, especially if a supervisor comes by.

It’s weird because I used to be pretty comfortable there. I mean, warehouse noise never bothered me much—forklifts, beepers, guys yelling across aisles, all that. Now even normal questions can feel like they’re pointing out I’m behind. Then I go home completely drained, and I’m not even doing basketball or sketching like I used to.

It’s like I’m carrying the warehouse home with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s mostly tied to work, I think. Like, I’ll be at home and still kind of carrying it with me, but the real “on edge” feeling starts before I even get into the warehouse.\n\nEver since they made me acting workflow lead, I walk in tense, like I’m waiting for the next thing to go wrong. And then stupid stuff—SAP errors, little things I used to handle—sets me off. I don’t like that. I’ve even been missing cycle-count deadlines, which isn’t like me.\n\nAt home it’s less like panic or something and more like I’m just drained. I used to go play basketball after work, or sit down and sketch for a bit—my brother used to joke my sketches all looked like warehouse layouts, actually—but lately I just don’t have the energy. So… yeah, not exactly all the time outside work, but it follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you became acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time. Down, maybe? But it’s kind of hard to separate from just being worn out, you know?\n\nLike, my buddy asked me the other day if I wanted to catch a game, and I started thinking about parking and traffic and how late it would be, and then somehow I’m thinking about the receiving dock at work again. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around crying or anything. It’s more like everything feels heavier since that role got put on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m avoiding them exactly… I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do the job. But some stuff is definitely slipping.\n\nLike the cycle-count deadlines—my supervisor actually brought that up in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I’ve always been the guy who stays on top of that, so having it pointed out felt… bad.\n\nAnd then after work, it’s like I don’t switch back into being myself. I’ve stopped showing up for basketball, and I don’t really sit down to sketch like I used to. It’s not work tasks, I guess, but work is kind of taking over everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Detached… like, watching myself from outside or something? I don’t think so. Not really.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll kind of zone out for a second when there’s too much going on, like the scanner’s beeping and someone’s asking me about a pallet location, and I’m thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. That might just be me being distracted. My brother does this thing where he walks into a room and forgets why he’s there—he says it’s age, but he’s only two years older than me, so…\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed feeling unreal or like I’m not in my body or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Significant weight? I don’t think so. I mean, my pants still fit the same, as far as I can tell.\n\nI haven’t really been weighing myself, though. We used to have this scale in the bathroom, but the battery died and I just never replaced it—one of those little things you keep meaning to do and then forget. But no, I haven’t had anyone say I look like I’ve lost weight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never had that feeling like I’m floating above myself or watching myself like a movie or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll get caught up in my head sometimes, but that’s different, right? Like when you’re driving and suddenly you’re at the exit and you’re like, “Oh, I guess I drove here.” My dad used to call that highway hypnosis—he’d say it every time we went on long trips, which got old fast. But, no, I don’t feel outside my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things that happen at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say worthless. Guilty… maybe sometimes? Like if something gets messed up at work, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder if I should’ve caught it sooner.\n\nBut I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more like… I get stuck on whether I handled something the right way. My mom used to do that too, actually—she’d check the stove three times before leaving, and then we’d be halfway to my aunt’s and she’d ask if she turned it off. Not the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe I feel bad about stuff sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d call it guilty or worthless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any activities outside of work that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I am. I used to play basketball after work with a couple guys, and I just haven’t been showing up. At first it was like, “I’ll go next week,” but then next week I’m still thinking about work stuff or staying late.\n\nAnd I used to sketch a lot too—nothing fancy, just kind of a way to shut my brain off. I haven’t really made time for that either. Work’s kind of… taking over everything right now. Even my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so then I feel like if I’m not focused on work, I’m falling further behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're interacting with your coworkers or supervisors at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with supervisors, I think. Coworkers too sometimes, but it’s more like when I walk into the warehouse now, I’m already tense before anything even happens.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m on edge all the time there. Like if someone puts something in wrong in SAP, even a little error, I’ll snap more than I mean to. And then I’m thinking about cycle counts I missed—stuff I didn’t used to miss before—and it just makes talking to people feel loaded, especially if a supervisor comes by.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to be pretty comfortable there. I mean, warehouse noise never bothered me much—forklifts, beepers, guys yelling across aisles, all that. Now even normal questions can feel like they’re pointing out I’m behind. Then I go home completely drained, and I’m not even doing basketball or sketching like I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… definitely. Little things at work are getting to me way more than they used to.

Like, if someone makes a small SAP error, I’ll get irritated and snap, and then afterward I’m like, why did I react like that? Before, I would’ve just fixed it or shown them how to do it. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I walk into the warehouse already tense.

It’s funny, because I used to be pretty steady there. I mean, not funny, but… you know. I’d deal with the usual chaos, shipments coming in, people asking questions, whatever. Now if I miss a cycle-count deadline—which I didn’t really used to do—it just feels like this whole thing piling up on me.

And then I go home and I’m just drained. I don’t even go play basketball or sit down and sketch like I used to. So, yeah, stuff that wouldn’t have bothered me before is bothering me now.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I’m here because work’s been getting to me in a way it didn’t before. They made me the acting workflow lead in the warehouse, and ever since then I walk in already tense, like I’m bracing for something.\n\nAnd it’s stupid little things sometimes—like SAP errors, stuff I used to just fix and move on from—I’ll snap about them now. I’m missing cycle-count deadlines too, which… I didn’t used to do. I’ve always been pretty solid with that kind of thing.\n\nThen I get home and I’m just wiped out. I used to play basketball after work or mess around with my sketches, but lately I don’t even have it in me. It’s like I’m carrying the warehouse home with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s mostly tied to work, I think. Like, I’ll be at home and still kind of carrying it with me, but the real “on edge” feeling starts before I even get into the warehouse.\n\nEver since they made me acting workflow lead, I walk in tense, like I’m waiting for the next thing to go wrong. And then stupid stuff—SAP errors, little things I used to handle—sets me off. I don’t like that. I’ve even been missing cycle-count deadlines, which isn’t like me.\n\nAt home it’s less like panic or something and more like I’m just drained. I used to go play basketball after work, or sit down and sketch for a bit—my brother used to joke my sketches all looked like warehouse layouts, actually—but lately I just don’t have the energy. So… yeah, not exactly all the time outside work, but it follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you became acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time. Down, maybe? But it’s kind of hard to separate from just being worn out, you know?\n\nLike, my buddy asked me the other day if I wanted to catch a game, and I started thinking about parking and traffic and how late it would be, and then somehow I’m thinking about the receiving dock at work again. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around crying or anything. It’s more like everything feels heavier since that role got put on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m avoiding them exactly… I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do the job. But some stuff is definitely slipping.\n\nLike the cycle-count deadlines—my supervisor actually brought that up in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I’ve always been the guy who stays on top of that, so having it pointed out felt… bad.\n\nAnd then after work, it’s like I don’t switch back into being myself. I’ve stopped showing up for basketball, and I don’t really sit down to sketch like I used to. It’s not work tasks, I guess, but work is kind of taking over everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Detached… like, watching myself from outside or something? I don’t think so. Not really.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll kind of zone out for a second when there’s too much going on, like the scanner’s beeping and someone’s asking me about a pallet location, and I’m thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. That might just be me being distracted. My brother does this thing where he walks into a room and forgets why he’s there—he says it’s age, but he’s only two years older than me, so…\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed feeling unreal or like I’m not in my body or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Significant weight? I don’t think so. I mean, my pants still fit the same, as far as I can tell.\n\nI haven’t really been weighing myself, though. We used to have this scale in the bathroom, but the battery died and I just never replaced it—one of those little things you keep meaning to do and then forget. But no, I haven’t had anyone say I look like I’ve lost weight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never had that feeling like I’m floating above myself or watching myself like a movie or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll get caught up in my head sometimes, but that’s different, right? Like when you’re driving and suddenly you’re at the exit and you’re like, “Oh, I guess I drove here.” My dad used to call that highway hypnosis—he’d say it every time we went on long trips, which got old fast. But, no, I don’t feel outside my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things that happen at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say worthless. Guilty… maybe sometimes? Like if something gets messed up at work, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder if I should’ve caught it sooner.\n\nBut I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more like… I get stuck on whether I handled something the right way. My mom used to do that too, actually—she’d check the stove three times before leaving, and then we’d be halfway to my aunt’s and she’d ask if she turned it off. Not the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe I feel bad about stuff sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d call it guilty or worthless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any activities outside of work that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I am. I used to play basketball after work with a couple guys, and I just haven’t been showing up. At first it was like, “I’ll go next week,” but then next week I’m still thinking about work stuff or staying late.\n\nAnd I used to sketch a lot too—nothing fancy, just kind of a way to shut my brain off. I haven’t really made time for that either. Work’s kind of… taking over everything right now. Even my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so then I feel like if I’m not focused on work, I’m falling further behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're interacting with your coworkers or supervisors at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with supervisors, I think. Coworkers too sometimes, but it’s more like when I walk into the warehouse now, I’m already tense before anything even happens.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m on edge all the time there. Like if someone puts something in wrong in SAP, even a little error, I’ll snap more than I mean to. And then I’m thinking about cycle counts I missed—stuff I didn’t used to miss before—and it just makes talking to people feel loaded, especially if a supervisor comes by.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to be pretty comfortable there. I mean, warehouse noise never bothered me much—forklifts, beepers, guys yelling across aisles, all that. Now even normal questions can feel like they’re pointing out I’m behind. Then I go home completely drained, and I’m not even doing basketball or sketching like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting upset or emotional over things at work that wouldn't have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. Little things at work are getting to me way more than they used to.\n\nLike, if someone makes a small SAP error, I’ll get irritated and snap, and then afterward I’m like, why did I react like that? Before, I would’ve just fixed it or shown them how to do it. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I walk into the warehouse already tense.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be pretty steady there. I mean, not funny, but… you know. I’d deal with the usual chaos, shipments coming in, people asking questions, whatever. Now if I miss a cycle-count deadline—which I didn’t really used to do—it just feels like this whole thing piling up on me.\n\nAnd then I go home and I’m just drained. I don’t even go play basketball or sit down and sketch like I used to. So, yeah, stuff that wouldn’t have bothered me before is bothering me now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know… I’m not sure I’d say I can’t feel happy. Like, if something good happens, I can still recognize it’s good.

But I guess I don’t really think about it that way. I’ve been more caught up in just getting through the day, and then there’s always some random work thing in my head. Like the other day I was supposed to meet my cousin for lunch and I spent half the time talking about pallet locations, which is ridiculous because he doesn’t even work in a warehouse.

So… less excitement? Maybe. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. It’s more like I’m distracted or worn down, not that I never feel anything positive.

Yeah… I mean, I guess so. I used to actually look forward to basketball after work, even if I was tired. Same with sketching—like I’d sit down and mess around for an hour, and it helped me reset.

But no, I haven’t had anyone say I look like I’ve lost weight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never had that feeling like I’m floating above myself or watching myself like a movie or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll get caught up in my head sometimes, but that’s different, right? Like when you’re driving and suddenly you’re at the exit and you’re like, “Oh, I guess I drove here.” My dad used to call that highway hypnosis—he’d say it every time we went on long trips, which got old fast. But, no, I don’t feel outside my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things that happen at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say worthless. Guilty… maybe sometimes? Like if something gets messed up at work, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder if I should’ve caught it sooner.\n\nBut I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more like… I get stuck on whether I handled something the right way. My mom used to do that too, actually—she’d check the stove three times before leaving, and then we’d be halfway to my aunt’s and she’d ask if she turned it off. Not the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe I feel bad about stuff sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d call it guilty or worthless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any activities outside of work that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I am. I used to play basketball after work with a couple guys, and I just haven’t been showing up. At first it was like, “I’ll go next week,” but then next week I’m still thinking about work stuff or staying late.\n\nAnd I used to sketch a lot too—nothing fancy, just kind of a way to shut my brain off. I haven’t really made time for that either. Work’s kind of… taking over everything right now. Even my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so then I feel like if I’m not focused on work, I’m falling further behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're interacting with your coworkers or supervisors at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with supervisors, I think. Coworkers too sometimes, but it’s more like when I walk into the warehouse now, I’m already tense before anything even happens.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m on edge all the time there. Like if someone puts something in wrong in SAP, even a little error, I’ll snap more than I mean to. And then I’m thinking about cycle counts I missed—stuff I didn’t used to miss before—and it just makes talking to people feel loaded, especially if a supervisor comes by.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to be pretty comfortable there. I mean, warehouse noise never bothered me much—forklifts, beepers, guys yelling across aisles, all that. Now even normal questions can feel like they’re pointing out I’m behind. Then I go home completely drained, and I’m not even doing basketball or sketching like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting upset or emotional over things at work that wouldn't have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. Little things at work are getting to me way more than they used to.\n\nLike, if someone makes a small SAP error, I’ll get irritated and snap, and then afterward I’m like, why did I react like that? Before, I would’ve just fixed it or shown them how to do it. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I walk into the warehouse already tense.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be pretty steady there. I mean, not funny, but… you know. I’d deal with the usual chaos, shipments coming in, people asking questions, whatever. Now if I miss a cycle-count deadline—which I didn’t really used to do—it just feels like this whole thing piling up on me.\n\nAnd then I go home and I’m just drained. I don’t even go play basketball or sit down and sketch like I used to. So, yeah, stuff that wouldn’t have bothered me before is bothering me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're less able to experience positive emotions, like happiness or excitement, than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I’d say I can’t feel happy. Like, if something good happens, I can still recognize it’s good.\n\nBut I guess I don’t really think about it that way. I’ve been more caught up in just getting through the day, and then there’s always some random work thing in my head. Like the other day I was supposed to meet my cousin for lunch and I spent half the time talking about pallet locations, which is ridiculous because he doesn’t even work in a warehouse.\n\nSo… less excitement? Maybe. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. It’s more like I’m distracted or worn down, not that I never feel anything positive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. I used to actually look forward to basketball after work, even if I was tired. Same with sketching—like I’d sit down and mess around for an hour, and it helped me reset.\n\nLately I just… don’t show up. I keep telling myself I’ll go next week, or I’ll draw on Sunday, but then work stuff is in my head, and it feels like the whole day is already used up. It’s kind of embarrassing, actually, because I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff.\n\nAnd then at work, my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so that’s been sitting with me too. It’s like work is spilling over into everything, and the things I used to enjoy are just getting pushed out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, kind of. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m trying to cut people off or anything, but I’ve definitely stopped showing up for stuff.

Like basketball—those guys text, and I’ll think, yeah, I should go, and then I just don’t. Same with sketching, that’s not exactly social all the time, but it was part of my normal life, you know? And now it’s like work has just spread into everything.

My supervisor had to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, and ever since then it’s been stuck in my head. So if someone outside work wants to talk or do something, I’m either distracted or I just feel like I don’t have the energy. It’s not that I don’t like them. I just… don’t show up the way I used to.

Like when you’re driving and suddenly you’re at the exit and you’re like, “Oh, I guess I drove here.” My dad used to call that highway hypnosis—he’d say it every time we went on long trips, which got old fast. But, no, I don’t feel outside my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things that happen at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say worthless. Guilty… maybe sometimes? Like if something gets messed up at work, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder if I should’ve caught it sooner.\n\nBut I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more like… I get stuck on whether I handled something the right way. My mom used to do that too, actually—she’d check the stove three times before leaving, and then we’d be halfway to my aunt’s and she’d ask if she turned it off. Not the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe I feel bad about stuff sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d call it guilty or worthless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any activities outside of work that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I am. I used to play basketball after work with a couple guys, and I just haven’t been showing up. At first it was like, “I’ll go next week,” but then next week I’m still thinking about work stuff or staying late.\n\nAnd I used to sketch a lot too—nothing fancy, just kind of a way to shut my brain off. I haven’t really made time for that either. Work’s kind of… taking over everything right now. Even my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so then I feel like if I’m not focused on work, I’m falling further behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're interacting with your coworkers or supervisors at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with supervisors, I think. Coworkers too sometimes, but it’s more like when I walk into the warehouse now, I’m already tense before anything even happens.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m on edge all the time there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. I used to be pretty regular with basketball, and I’d usually make time to sketch, even if it was just a little bit. Lately I just don’t go, or I keep putting it off.

It’s like work follows me home. My supervisor brought up the missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, and since then that’s been rattling around in my head. I’ll be sitting there with my sketchbook, or I’ll get a text about basketball, and instead I’m thinking about inventory stuff or what I messed up.

So yeah… I’ve kind of stopped showing up for the things I used to actually enjoy.

They made me the acting workflow lead in the warehouse, and ever since then I walk in already tense, like I’m bracing for something.\n\nAnd it’s stupid little things sometimes—like SAP errors, stuff I used to just fix and move on from—I’ll snap about them now. I’m missing cycle-count deadlines too, which… I didn’t used to do. I’ve always been pretty solid with that kind of thing.\n\nThen I get home and I’m just wiped out. I used to play basketball after work or mess around with my sketches, but lately I don’t even have it in me. Like, I’ll be at home and still kind of carrying it with me, but the real “on edge” feeling starts before I even get into the warehouse.\n\nEver since they made me acting workflow lead, I walk in tense, like I’m waiting for the next thing to go wrong. And then stupid stuff—SAP errors, little things I used to handle—sets me off. I don’t like that. I’ve even been missing cycle-count deadlines, which isn’t like me.\n\nAt home it’s less like panic or something and more like I’m just drained. I used to go play basketball after work, or sit down and sketch for a bit—my brother used to joke my sketches all looked like warehouse layouts, actually—but lately I just don’t have the energy. So… yeah, not exactly all the time outside work, but it follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you became acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time. Down, maybe? But it’s kind of hard to separate from just being worn out, you know?\n\nLike, my buddy asked me the other day if I wanted to catch a game, and I started thinking about parking and traffic and how late it would be, and then somehow I’m thinking about the receiving dock at work again. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around crying or anything. It’s more like everything feels heavier since that role got put on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m avoiding them exactly… I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do the job. I’ve stopped showing up for basketball, and I don’t really sit down to sketch like I used to. It’s not work tasks, I guess, but work is kind of taking over everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Detached… like, watching myself from outside or something? I don’t think so. Not really.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll kind of zone out for a second when there’s too much going on, like the scanner’s beeping and someone’s asking me about a pallet location, and I’m thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. That might just be me being distracted. My brother does this thing where he walks into a room and forgets why he’s there—he says it’s age, but he’s only two years older than me, so…\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed feeling unreal or like I’m not in my body or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Significant weight? I don’t think so. I mean, my pants still fit the same, as far as I can tell.\n\nI haven’t really been weighing myself, though. We used to have this scale in the bathroom, but the battery died and I just never replaced it—one of those little things you keep meaning to do and then forget. But no, I haven’t had anyone say I look like I’ve lost weight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never had that feeling like I’m floating above myself or watching myself like a movie or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll get caught up in my head sometimes, but that’s different, right? Like when you’re driving and suddenly you’re at the exit and you’re like, “Oh, I guess I drove here.” My dad used to call that highway hypnosis—he’d say it every time we went on long trips, which got old fast. But, no, I don’t feel outside my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things that happen at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say worthless. Guilty… maybe sometimes? Like if something gets messed up at work, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder if I should’ve caught it sooner.\n\nBut I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more like… I get stuck on whether I handled something the right way. My mom used to do that too, actually—she’d check the stove three times before leaving, and then we’d be halfway to my aunt’s and she’d ask if she turned it off. Not the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe I feel bad about stuff sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d call it guilty or worthless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any activities outside of work that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I am. I used to play basketball after work with a couple guys, and I just haven’t been showing up. I haven’t really made time for that either. Work’s kind of… taking over everything right now. Even my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so then I feel like if I’m not focused on work, I’m falling further behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're interacting with your coworkers or supervisors at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with supervisors, I think. Coworkers too sometimes, but it’s more like when I walk into the warehouse now, I’m already tense before anything even happens.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m on edge all the time there. Like if someone puts something in wrong in SAP, even a little error, I’ll snap more than I mean to. And then I’m thinking about cycle counts I missed—stuff I didn’t used to miss before—and it just makes talking to people feel loaded, especially if a supervisor comes by.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to be pretty comfortable there. Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to say it that dramatically, like “failure,” but that’s kind of what it feels like some mornings.

Ever since they made me acting workflow lead, I’ll walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And then it’ll be some small SAP error—something I probably would’ve handled fine before—and I snap or get short about it. Then I’m thinking about that all day.

They made me the acting workflow lead in the warehouse, and ever since then I walk in already tense, like I’m bracing for something.\n\nAnd it’s stupid little things sometimes—like SAP errors, stuff I used to just fix and move on from—I’ll snap about them now. I’m missing cycle-count deadlines too, which… I didn’t used to do. I’ve always been pretty solid with that kind of thing.\n\nThen I get home and I’m just wiped out. I used to play basketball after work or mess around with my sketches, but lately I don’t even have it in me. It’s like I’m carrying the warehouse home with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s mostly tied to work, I think. Like, I’ll be at home and still kind of carrying it with me, but the real “on edge” feeling starts before I even get into the warehouse.\n\nEver since they made me acting workflow lead, I walk in tense, like I’m waiting for the next thing to go wrong. And then stupid stuff—SAP errors, little things I used to handle—sets me off. I don’t like that. I’ve even been missing cycle-count deadlines, which isn’t like me.\n\nAt home it’s less like panic or something and more like I’m just drained. I used to go play basketball after work, or sit down and sketch for a bit—my brother used to joke my sketches all looked like warehouse layouts, actually—but lately I just don’t have the energy. So… yeah, not exactly all the time outside work, but it follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you became acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time. Down, maybe? But it’s kind of hard to separate from just being worn out, you know?\n\nLike, my buddy asked me the other day if I wanted to catch a game, and I started thinking about parking and traffic and how late it would be, and then somehow I’m thinking about the receiving dock at work again. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around crying or anything. It’s more like everything feels heavier since that role got put on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m avoiding them exactly… I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do the job. But some stuff is definitely slipping.\n\nLike the cycle-count deadlines—my supervisor actually brought that up in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I’ve always been the guy who stays on top of that, so having it pointed out felt… bad.\n\nAnd then after work, it’s like I don’t switch back into being myself. I’ve stopped showing up for basketball, and I don’t really sit down to sketch like I used to. It’s not work tasks, I guess, but work is kind of taking over everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Detached… like, watching myself from outside or something? I don’t think so. Not really.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll kind of zone out for a second when there’s too much going on, like the scanner’s beeping and someone’s asking me about a pallet location, and I’m thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. That might just be me being distracted. My brother does this thing where he walks into a room and forgets why he’s there—he says it’s age, but he’s only two years older than me, so…\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed feeling unreal or like I’m not in my body or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Significant weight? I don’t think so. I mean, my pants still fit the same, as far as I can tell.\n\nI haven’t really been weighing myself, though. We used to have this scale in the bathroom, but the battery died and I just never replaced it—one of those little things you keep meaning to do and then forget. But no, I haven’t had anyone say I look like I’ve lost weight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never had that feeling like I’m floating above myself or watching myself like a movie or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll get caught up in my head sometimes, but that’s different, right? Like when you’re driving and suddenly you’re at the exit and you’re like, “Oh, I guess I drove here.” My dad used to call that highway hypnosis—he’d say it every time we went on long trips, which got old fast. But, no, I don’t feel outside my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things that happen at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say worthless. Guilty… maybe sometimes? Like if something gets messed up at work, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder if I should’ve caught it sooner.\n\nBut I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more like… I get stuck on whether I handled something the right way. My mom used to do that too, actually—she’d check the stove three times before leaving, and then we’d be halfway to my aunt’s and she’d ask if she turned it off. Not the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe I feel bad about stuff sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d call it guilty or worthless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any activities outside of work that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I am. I used to play basketball after work with a couple guys, and I just haven’t been showing up. At first it was like, “I’ll go next week,” but then next week I’m still thinking about work stuff or staying late.\n\nAnd I used to sketch a lot too—nothing fancy, just kind of a way to shut my brain off. I haven’t really made time for that either. Work’s kind of… taking over everything right now. Even my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so then I feel like if I’m not focused on work, I’m falling further behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're interacting with your coworkers or supervisors at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with supervisors, I think. Coworkers too sometimes, but it’s more like when I walk into the warehouse now, I’m already tense before anything even happens.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m on edge all the time there. Like if someone puts something in wrong in SAP, even a little error, I’ll snap more than I mean to. And then I’m thinking about cycle counts I missed—stuff I didn’t used to miss before—and it just makes talking to people feel loaded, especially if a supervisor comes by.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to be pretty comfortable there. I mean, warehouse noise never bothered me much—forklifts, beepers, guys yelling across aisles, all that. Now even normal questions can feel like they’re pointing out I’m behind. Then I go home completely drained, and I’m not even doing basketball or sketching like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting upset or emotional over things at work that wouldn't have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. Little things at work are getting to me way more than they used to.\n\nLike, if someone makes a small SAP error, I’ll get irritated and snap, and then afterward I’m like, why did I react like that? Before, I would’ve just fixed it or shown them how to do it. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I walk into the warehouse already tense.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be pretty steady there. I mean, not funny, but… you know. I’d deal with the usual chaos, shipments coming in, people asking questions, whatever. Now if I miss a cycle-count deadline—which I didn’t really used to do—it just feels like this whole thing piling up on me.\n\nAnd then I go home and I’m just drained. I don’t even go play basketball or sit down and sketch like I used to. So, yeah, stuff that wouldn’t have bothered me before is bothering me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're less able to experience positive emotions, like happiness or excitement, than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I’d say I can’t feel happy. Like, if something good happens, I can still recognize it’s good.\n\nBut I guess I don’t really think about it that way. I’ve been more caught up in just getting through the day, and then there’s always some random work thing in my head. Like the other day I was supposed to meet my cousin for lunch and I spent half the time talking about pallet locations, which is ridiculous because he doesn’t even work in a warehouse.\n\nSo… less excitement? Maybe. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. It’s more like I’m distracted or worn down, not that I never feel anything positive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. I used to actually look forward to basketball after work, even if I was tired. Same with sketching—like I’d sit down and mess around for an hour, and it helped me reset.\n\nLately I just… don’t show up. I keep telling myself I’ll go next week, or I’ll draw on Sunday, but then work stuff is in my head, and it feels like the whole day is already used up. It’s kind of embarrassing, actually, because I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff.\n\nAnd then at work, my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so that’s been sitting with me too. I just… don’t show up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Lately I just don’t go, or I keep putting it off.\n\nIt’s like work follows me home. Like when you’re driving and suddenly you’re at the exit and you’re like, “Oh, I guess I drove here.” My dad used to call that highway hypnosis—he’d say it every time we went on long trips, which got old fast. But, no, I don’t feel outside my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things that happen at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say worthless. Guilty… maybe sometimes? Like if something gets messed up at work, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder if I should’ve caught it sooner.\n\nBut I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more like… I get stuck on whether I handled something the right way. My mom used to do that too, actually—she’d check the stove three times before leaving, and then we’d be halfway to my aunt’s and she’d ask if she turned it off. Not the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe I feel bad about stuff sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d call it guilty or worthless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any activities outside of work that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I am. I used to play basketball after work with a couple guys, and I just haven’t been showing up. At first it was like, “I’ll go next week,” but then next week I’m still thinking about work stuff or staying late.\n\nAnd I used to sketch a lot too—nothing fancy, just kind of a way to shut my brain off. I haven’t really made time for that either. Work’s kind of… taking over everything right now. Even my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so then I feel like if I’m not focused on work, I’m falling further behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're interacting with your coworkers or supervisors at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with supervisors, I think. Coworkers too sometimes, but it’s more like when I walk into the warehouse now, I’m already tense before anything even happens.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m on edge all the time there. Like if someone puts something in wrong in SAP, even a little error, I’ll snap more than I mean to. And then I’m thinking about cycle counts I missed—stuff I didn’t used to miss before—and it just makes talking to people feel loaded, especially if a supervisor comes by.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to be pretty comfortable there. I mean, warehouse noise never bothered me much—forklifts, beepers, guys yelling across aisles, all that. Now even normal questions can feel like they’re pointing out I’m behind. Then I go home completely drained, and I’m not even doing basketball or sketching like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting upset or emotional over things at work that wouldn't have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. Little things at work are getting to me way more than they used to.\n\nLike, if someone makes a small SAP error, I’ll get irritated and snap, and then afterward I’m like, why did I react like that? Before, I would’ve just fixed it or shown them how to do it. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I walk into the warehouse already tense.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be pretty steady there. I mean, not funny, but… you know. I’d deal with the usual chaos, shipments coming in, people asking questions, whatever. Now if I miss a cycle-count deadline—which I didn’t really used to do—it just feels like this whole thing piling up on me.\n\nAnd then I go home and I’m just drained. I don’t even go play basketball or sit down and sketch like I used to. So, yeah, stuff that wouldn’t have bothered me before is bothering me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're less able to experience positive emotions, like happiness or excitement, than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I’d say I can’t feel happy. Like, if something good happens, I can still recognize it’s good.\n\nBut I guess I don’t really think about it that way. I’ve been more caught up in just getting through the day, and then there’s always some random work thing in my head. Like the other day I was supposed to meet my cousin for lunch and I spent half the time talking about pallet locations, which is ridiculous because he doesn’t even work in a warehouse.\n\nSo… less excitement? Maybe. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. It’s more like I’m distracted or worn down, not that I never feel anything positive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. I used to actually look forward to basketball after work, even if I was tired. Same with sketching—like I’d sit down and mess around for an hour, and it helped me reset.\n\nLately I just… don’t show up. I keep telling myself I’ll go next week, or I’ll draw on Sunday, but then work stuff is in my head, and it feels like the whole day is already used up. It’s kind of embarrassing, actually, because I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff.\n\nAnd then at work, my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so that’s been sitting with me too. It’s like work is spilling over into everything, and the things I used to enjoy are just getting pushed out.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or conversations with people outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m trying to cut people off or anything, but I’ve definitely stopped showing up for stuff.\n\nLike basketball—those guys text, and I’ll think, yeah, I should go, and then I just don’t. Same with sketching, that’s not exactly social all the time, but it was part of my normal life, you know? And now it’s like work has just spread into everything.\n\nMy supervisor had to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, and ever since then it’s been stuck in my head. So if someone outside work wants to talk or do something, I’m either distracted or I just feel like I don’t have the energy. It’s not that I don’t like them. I just… don’t show up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I used to be pretty regular with basketball, and I’d usually make time to sketch, even if it was just a little bit. Lately I just don’t go, or I keep putting it off.\n\nIt’s like work follows me home. My supervisor brought up the missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, and since then that’s been rattling around in my head. I’ll be sitting there with my sketchbook, or I’ll get a text about basketball, and instead I’m thinking about inventory stuff or what I messed up.\n\nSo yeah… I’ve kind of stopped showing up for the things I used to actually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're inadequate or a failure in your role as acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to say it that dramatically, like “failure,” but that’s kind of what it feels like some mornings.\n\nEver since they made me acting workflow lead, I’ll walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And then it’ll be some small SAP error—something I probably would’ve handled fine before—and I snap or get short about it. Then I’m thinking about that all day.\n\nAnd the cycle-count deadlines… those are the ones that really bother me because I didn’t used to miss that kind of thing. I used to be on top of it. Now I miss them, and then I go home drained, and instead of basketball or sketching, I’m just sitting there replaying what I messed up.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I do feel inadequate in it. Like maybe they picked the wrong person.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It pretty much started when they merged the inventory teams and put me in that acting workflow lead spot.\n\nI remember it because everything kind of changed at once—new responsibilities, different people asking me things, and I was still trying to do my regular work too. And, I mean, four months doesn’t sound that long when you say it, but it feels like it’s been… a lot longer, honestly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll be sitting there with my sketchbook, or I’ll get a text about basketball, and instead I’m thinking about inventory stuff or what I messed up.\n\nSo yeah… I’ve kind of stopped showing up for the things I used to actually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're inadequate or a failure in your role as acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to say it that dramatically, like “failure,” but that’s kind of what it feels like some mornings.\n\nEver since they made me acting workflow lead, I’ll walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And then it’ll be some small SAP error—something I probably would’ve handled fine before—and I snap or get short about it. Then I’m thinking about that all day.\n\nAnd the cycle-count deadlines… those are the ones that really bother me because I didn’t used to miss that kind of thing. I used to be on top of it. Now I miss them, and then I go home drained, and instead of basketball or sketching, I’m just sitting there replaying what I messed up.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I do feel inadequate in it. Like maybe they picked the wrong person.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It pretty much started when they merged the inventory teams and put me in that acting workflow lead spot.\n\nI remember it because everything kind of changed at once—new responsibilities, different people asking me things, and I was still trying to do my regular work too. And, I mean, four months doesn’t sound that long when you say it, but it feels like it’s been… a lot longer, honestly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself avoiding places or situations outside of work because you're afraid of feeling overwhelmed or anxious?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / shipping_receiving_or_inventory_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it like I’m avoiding places because I’m scared exactly. I mean… I don’t really go out as much, but that might be more because I’m tired or I just don’t feel like dealing with plans.

Like, if someone asks me to go somewhere after work, I’ll think about the parking, or whether I’m going to be bad company, and then I’ll just stay home. But I don’t know if that’s “afraid of feeling overwhelmed” or just… I don’t have the energy.

Sorry, I’m probably not answering that straight. I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding specific places, no.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I’m here because work’s been getting to me in a way it didn’t before. They made me the acting workflow lead in the warehouse, and ever since then I walk in already tense, like I’m bracing for something.\n\nAnd it’s stupid little things sometimes—like SAP errors, stuff I used to just fix and move on from—I’ll snap about them now. I’m missing cycle-count deadlines too, which… I didn’t used to do. I’ve always been pretty solid with that kind of thing.\n\nThen I get home and I’m just wiped out. I used to play basketball after work or mess around with my sketches, but lately I don’t even have it in me. It’s like I’m carrying the warehouse home with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s mostly tied to work, I think. Like, I’ll be at home and still kind of carrying it with me, but the real “on edge” feeling starts before I even get into the warehouse.\n\nEver since they made me acting workflow lead, I walk in tense, like I’m waiting for the next thing to go wrong. And then stupid stuff—SAP errors, little things I used to handle—sets me off. I don’t like that. I’ve even been missing cycle-count deadlines, which isn’t like me.\n\nAt home it’s less like panic or something and more like I’m just drained. I used to go play basketball after work, or sit down and sketch for a bit—my brother used to joke my sketches all looked like warehouse layouts, actually—but lately I just don’t have the energy. So… yeah, not exactly all the time outside work, but it follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you became acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the time. Down, maybe? But it’s kind of hard to separate from just being worn out, you know?\n\nLike, my buddy asked me the other day if I wanted to catch a game, and I started thinking about parking and traffic and how late it would be, and then somehow I’m thinking about the receiving dock at work again. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around crying or anything. It’s more like everything feels heavier since that role got put on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m avoiding them exactly… I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do the job. But some stuff is definitely slipping.\n\nLike the cycle-count deadlines—my supervisor actually brought that up in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I’ve always been the guy who stays on top of that, so having it pointed out felt… bad.\n\nAnd then after work, it’s like I don’t switch back into being myself. I’ve stopped showing up for basketball, and I don’t really sit down to sketch like I used to. It’s not work tasks, I guess, but work is kind of taking over everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Detached… like, watching myself from outside or something? I don’t think so. Not really.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll kind of zone out for a second when there’s too much going on, like the scanner’s beeping and someone’s asking me about a pallet location, and I’m thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. That might just be me being distracted. My brother does this thing where he walks into a room and forgets why he’s there—he says it’s age, but he’s only two years older than me, so…\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed feeling unreal or like I’m not in my body or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since becoming acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Significant weight? I don’t think so. I mean, my pants still fit the same, as far as I can tell.\n\nI haven’t really been weighing myself, though. We used to have this scale in the bathroom, but the battery died and I just never replaced it—one of those little things you keep meaning to do and then forget. But no, I haven’t had anyone say I look like I’ve lost weight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never had that feeling like I’m floating above myself or watching myself like a movie or anything.\n\nI mean, I’ll get caught up in my head sometimes, but that’s different, right? Like when you’re driving and suddenly you’re at the exit and you’re like, “Oh, I guess I drove here.” My dad used to call that highway hypnosis—he’d say it every time we went on long trips, which got old fast. But, no, I don’t feel outside my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things that happen at work or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say worthless. Guilty… maybe sometimes? Like if something gets messed up at work, I’ll replay it in my head and wonder if I should’ve caught it sooner.\n\nBut I don’t sit there thinking I’m a terrible person or anything. It’s more like… I get stuck on whether I handled something the right way. My mom used to do that too, actually—she’d check the stove three times before leaving, and then we’d be halfway to my aunt’s and she’d ask if she turned it off. Not the same thing, I guess.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe I feel bad about stuff sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d call it guilty or worthless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any activities outside of work that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I am. I used to play basketball after work with a couple guys, and I just haven’t been showing up. At first it was like, “I’ll go next week,” but then next week I’m still thinking about work stuff or staying late.\n\nAnd I used to sketch a lot too—nothing fancy, just kind of a way to shut my brain off. I haven’t really made time for that either. Work’s kind of… taking over everything right now. Even my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so then I feel like if I’m not focused on work, I’m falling further behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're interacting with your coworkers or supervisors at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with supervisors, I think. Coworkers too sometimes, but it’s more like when I walk into the warehouse now, I’m already tense before anything even happens.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m on edge all the time there. Like if someone puts something in wrong in SAP, even a little error, I’ll snap more than I mean to. And then I’m thinking about cycle counts I missed—stuff I didn’t used to miss before—and it just makes talking to people feel loaded, especially if a supervisor comes by.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to be pretty comfortable there. I mean, warehouse noise never bothered me much—forklifts, beepers, guys yelling across aisles, all that. Now even normal questions can feel like they’re pointing out I’m behind. Then I go home completely drained, and I’m not even doing basketball or sketching like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting upset or emotional over things at work that wouldn't have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. Little things at work are getting to me way more than they used to.\n\nLike, if someone makes a small SAP error, I’ll get irritated and snap, and then afterward I’m like, why did I react like that? Before, I would’ve just fixed it or shown them how to do it. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I walk into the warehouse already tense.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be pretty steady there. I mean, not funny, but… you know. I’d deal with the usual chaos, shipments coming in, people asking questions, whatever. Now if I miss a cycle-count deadline—which I didn’t really used to do—it just feels like this whole thing piling up on me.\n\nAnd then I go home and I’m just drained. I don’t even go play basketball or sit down and sketch like I used to. So, yeah, stuff that wouldn’t have bothered me before is bothering me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're less able to experience positive emotions, like happiness or excitement, than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I’d say I can’t feel happy. Like, if something good happens, I can still recognize it’s good.\n\nBut I guess I don’t really think about it that way. I’ve been more caught up in just getting through the day, and then there’s always some random work thing in my head. Like the other day I was supposed to meet my cousin for lunch and I spent half the time talking about pallet locations, which is ridiculous because he doesn’t even work in a warehouse.\n\nSo… less excitement? Maybe. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. It’s more like I’m distracted or worn down, not that I never feel anything positive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. I used to actually look forward to basketball after work, even if I was tired. Same with sketching—like I’d sit down and mess around for an hour, and it helped me reset.\n\nLately I just… don’t show up. I keep telling myself I’ll go next week, or I’ll draw on Sunday, but then work stuff is in my head, and it feels like the whole day is already used up. It’s kind of embarrassing, actually, because I used to be pretty consistent with that stuff.\n\nAnd then at work, my supervisor had to bring up missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, so that’s been sitting with me too. It’s like work is spilling over into everything, and the things I used to enjoy are just getting pushed out.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or conversations with people outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m trying to cut people off or anything, but I’ve definitely stopped showing up for stuff.\n\nLike basketball—those guys text, and I’ll think, yeah, I should go, and then I just don’t. Same with sketching, that’s not exactly social all the time, but it was part of my normal life, you know? And now it’s like work has just spread into everything.\n\nMy supervisor had to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, and ever since then it’s been stuck in my head. So if someone outside work wants to talk or do something, I’m either distracted or I just feel like I don’t have the energy. It’s not that I don’t like them. I just… don’t show up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I used to be pretty regular with basketball, and I’d usually make time to sketch, even if it was just a little bit. Lately I just don’t go, or I keep putting it off.\n\nIt’s like work follows me home. My supervisor brought up the missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, and since then that’s been rattling around in my head. I’ll be sitting there with my sketchbook, or I’ll get a text about basketball, and instead I’m thinking about inventory stuff or what I messed up.\n\nSo yeah… I’ve kind of stopped showing up for the things I used to actually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're inadequate or a failure in your role as acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to say it that dramatically, like “failure,” but that’s kind of what it feels like some mornings.\n\nEver since they made me acting workflow lead, I’ll walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And then it’ll be some small SAP error—something I probably would’ve handled fine before—and I snap or get short about it. Then I’m thinking about that all day.\n\nAnd the cycle-count deadlines… those are the ones that really bother me because I didn’t used to miss that kind of thing. I used to be on top of it. Now I miss them, and then I go home drained, and instead of basketball or sketching, I’m just sitting there replaying what I messed up.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I do feel inadequate in it. Like maybe they picked the wrong person.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It pretty much started when they merged the inventory teams and put me in that acting workflow lead spot.\n\nI remember it because everything kind of changed at once—new responsibilities, different people asking me things, and I was still trying to do my regular work too. And, I mean, four months doesn’t sound that long when you say it, but it feels like it’s been… a lot longer, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places or situations outside of work because you're afraid of feeling overwhelmed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it like I’m avoiding places because I’m scared exactly. I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding specific places, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I used to play basketball after work or mess around with my sketches, but lately I don’t even have it in me.
